<summary>This is the title page of an educational Latin textbook by John Amos Comenius, edited by Christopher Liebruder to include a Polish translation and detailed grammatical notes. It was printed in Königsberg and later became part of the Library of Saint Genevieve in Paris.</summary>

--- Page 6 ---
<meta>Continuity: This page follows the title page which introduced Christopher Liebruder’s Polish-Latin edition of Comenius’s foundational textbook, noting its provenance from the Library of Saint Genevieve.</meta>

<image-desc>At the top of the page are three decorative borders: a row of asterisks, followed by two rows of small, heart-shaped or leaf-shaped ornaments. Below the heading is an ornate woodcut drop cap 'V' showing a figure seated in an interior space, used to start the word 'Ut'.</image-desc>

->GREETINGS TO THE READERS. <note>original: "LECTORIBUS S." Short for "Lectoribus Salutem," a standard Latin greeting wishing the reader health.</note><-

Just as every house has its Doorway <note>original: "Januam"</note>, so every Doorway has its own Vestibule <note>original: "Vestibulum," an entrance hall or porch</note>: where it is permitted for one who has entered to pause, to contemplate the exterior of the house, to look through the doors at the atrium, and to wait until they are welcomed and led inside by the household. Therefore, it may not be out of place that it came to our mind to now build a *Vestibule* for the *Gate of Languages* <note>The *Janua Linguarum Reserata* (The Gate of Languages Unlocked) was Comenius's most famous textbook; this "Vestibule" serves as a simpler introduction to it.</note> which was recently constructed: and of this matter, we appoint you, *wise Reader*, to be the judge.

I had declared in the preface of that "Gate" of mine that it was prepared for *beginners in Latin* <note>original: "Latinitatis tironibus"</note>: and indeed, it was publicly received in that way. However, I truly think it is to be feared that for *Beginners* to be cast suddenly into such a vast forest of things and words might be too much. It would be safer for them to be held in the vestibule, and for the general features of the entire structure (that is, the primary hinges <note>original: "cardines," meaning the fundamental points or essential categories</note> of *Things and Words*) to be shown to them before they are let in to examine the particulars. With this plan, the present little work was written, so that it might be the first material for absolute beginners to practice *Declensions* <term>declinationes: the way nouns, pronouns, and adjectives change their endings to show their grammatical role</term> (at least the regular ones), and a way of access to the *Gate* itself.

<vocab>Doorway, Vestibule, Reader, Of Latinity, Beginners, Declensions, Little work</vocab>

<summary>In this preface, the author explains the purpose of the "Vestibule" as a simplified introductory textbook designed to prepare students for the more complex "Gate of Languages." He argues that beginners need a gradual introduction to Latin grammar and vocabulary before tackling advanced details.</summary>
<keywords>Comenius, Latin pedagogy, Vestibulum, Janua Linguarum, linguistics, classical education, beginners, grammar</keywords>

--- Page 7 ---
<meta>Continuity: This page continues the preface by Comenius, moving from the philosophical justification of the "Vestibule" to the specific pedagogical methods and the structure of the book. Catchword "Omnia" on the previous page suggests a transition to "all things" or "all parts" of the language being introduced here.</meta>

I hesitated indeed, whether it might not be better to offer some childish dialogues to the boys, in order to make Latin more familiar to them; but I retained the form of the GATE <note>original: "JANUÆ." This refers to his famous textbook *Janua Linguarum Reserata* (The Gate of Languages Unlocked).</note>, so that this preparation truly leads to the Gate, that is, the art of expressing things properly <note>original: "Res propriè exprimendi artificium." Comenius believed language should directly reflect the reality of things, rather than being mere "chatter."</note>. For in learning the Latin language, we must not first strive to have boys become accustomed to chattering in Latin (as has been preposterously done until now), but rather that they learn to understand each thing in its proper meaning. "But," you will say, "more attention is needed this way." So be it; let them therefore become accustomed to engaging with books with attention. And why is there a need for haste? They are boys; they must be led slowly; slowly, I say, but wisely. I shall say, therefore, what has been done here.

I have selected over a thousand of the most common words and arranged them into very short little sentences, most of them consisting of two words. In the first chapter, I have placed Names with Added Names, or (as they call them) *Substantives with Adjectives*; in the second chapter, *Nouns with Verbs*; in the third, I have arranged indeclinable particles: *Adverbs, Prepositions, and Numerals*; the more common *Conjunctions and Interjections* are partly scattered throughout. The remaining matters concerning things that exist and happen in the School, in the Home, and in the City (at least as many as seemed possible to be offered as a first taste) are located in chapters IV, V, and VI; and finally, certain brief precepts concerning Virtues are found in chapter VII.

<note>The vocabulary list below categorizes the parts of speech and concepts introduced in the text.</note>
<term>Gate</term> (Janua), <term>Latin language</term> (Latinitas), <term>Substantives/Nouns</term> (Substantiva), <term>Adjectives</term> (Adjectiva), <term>Nouns/Names</term> (Nomina), <term>Verbs</term> (Verba), <term>Adverbs</term> (Adverbia), <term>Prepositions</term> (Præpositiones), <term>Numerals</term> (Numeralia), <term>Conjunctions</term> (Conjunctiones), <term>Interjections</term> (Interjectiones), <term>Virtues</term> (Virtutibus)

<summary>In this part of the preface, the author explains his decision to avoid simple dialogues in favor of a structured approach that teaches the "proper meaning" of things. He outlines the organization of the book into seven chapters covering basic grammar (nouns, verbs, etc.) and everyday vocabulary for the school, home, and city.</summary>
<keywords>pedagogy, Latin education, grammar, parts of speech, vocabulary structure, Janua Linguarum, student attention</keywords>

--- Page 8 ---
<meta>Continuity: This page continues Comenius's preface to his introductory textbook, the "Vestibule." Having previously described the selection and arrangement of words into simple sentences, he now explains the linguistic principles behind his choices—prioritizing literal meaning and natural word order—and begins to outline specific instructions for how teachers should use the book with beginners.</meta>

Indeed, all words are used literally <note>original: "propriè." In 17th-century linguistics, this meant using a word in its primary, physical sense rather than metaphorically.</note>, and nothing is used figuratively. The word order here is for the most part natural (so that the Noun precedes, and the Adjective or Verb follows); however, in some places, for the sake of the German language (so that word might correspond to word in parallel), a slight transposition has been made. *For example: Learn the Latin Language <note>original Latin: "Discite Latinam Lingvam."</note> instead of the Language Latin <note>original Latin: "Lingvam Latinam."</note>.* Since nouns <term>Substantives: Nouns that stand on their own.</term> by their nature precede, I thought something should be granted to the custom of the Germans, which is changed only with difficulty. But also this: I have avoided Latin ellipses <note>An "ellipsis" is the omission of a word that is understood from context.</note>, lest they be a stumbling block to the boys. *For example: "in the spring" <note>original German and Latin: "im Frühling / in vere."</note>* I have written out fully, although the Latin preposition is more pleasantly hidden <note>Latin often omits the preposition "in" when talking about time, but Comenius includes it to help the students.</note>. Likewise, *In order that it may not fall* <note>original Latin: "Ut ne ruat."</note> instead of *Lest it fall* <note>original Latin: "ne ruat."</note>. For I thought that in every way one must serve the weakness [of the student], and one must babble along with those who babble <note>original: "cum balbutientibus eo usq; balbutiendum." This is a famous Comenian principle: teachers must meet children at their own level of development, even if it means using "baby talk" or simplified structures.</note>. Nor was any other way fitting. For whatever begins is unformed, in every matter; why should we wish it otherwise here?

->Now, something must be advised concerning the use of this<-
->Vestibule <note>original: "Vestibuli." This refers to the book itself, envisioned as the entrance hall to the "Gate" of the language.</note>.<-

I. From this, the first beginners of Latin <term>Latinitatis tirones: "Recruits" or novices in the study of the Latin language.</term> can be taught to read promptly and pronounce correctly, by having the entire little book read aloud, of course, once or twice in certain measured portions.

II. And so that the meanings of the words may gradually become known, let them always put the native-language reading first <note>original: "vernaculam lectionem." Comenius was a pioneer in using the student's mother tongue to teach a second language.</note>. *For example: Come here, children <note>original German: "Kompt her Kinder," Latin: "Venite pueri," and Polish: "podźcie dzieci."</note> etc.* For in this way, twice as...

<summary>Comenius explains that he has simplified Latin grammar and word order to match the students' native German, arguing that teachers must accommodate the "weakness" of beginners. He also introduces his method of teaching: reading the text aloud and always starting with the student's native language.</summary>
<keywords>pedagogy, word order, literal meaning, ellipsis, beginners, native language, bilingual education, John Amos Comenius</keywords>

--- Page 9 ---
<meta>Continuity: This page continues Comenius's instructions for teachers. Having just argued that Latin grammar should be simplified and made more natural for young students, he now addresses how to link Latin words with the students' native language and outlines the progression of memory exercises and noun-adjective pairings.</meta>

(3)

Latin words will stick more easily if they are joined to the native words <note>original: "vernaculis." This refers to the student's mother tongue, such as German or Czech.</note> everywhere. [ What is handed down here by the Author concerning the placing of native words before their Latin equivalents—as also on the following page in section 4 regarding the method of drilling <term>Declinations: The way nouns, pronouns, and adjectives change their endings to show their role in a sentence (like "he" becoming "him").</term> into the boys—should be taken with a grain of salt <note>original: "cum grano salis." Even in the 17th century, this idiom was used to suggest caution or moderation.</note>: for just as this advice has its place only in reading (and not in the formal explanation of the text), so in what follows, the common method of declining using the technical names of the cases is more safely preferred. ]

III. Once the little book has been gone through twice in this way, let them proceed to the exercise of memory. Therefore, let them learn two or three little sentences every hour, and at the end of the hour, let them recite them from memory.

IV. At the same time, let an exercise in declining be added: the first chapter will supply the material for this, provided a simple method is followed: namely, that,

1. The noun <term>Substantive: A noun that stands on its own as a person, place, or thing.</term> alone should be taken.
2. It should be declined together with an Adjective, and at first indeed those of the same Declension: starting from the first: (*as Latin Language, Beautiful Language, Lowest Earth, Nearby Cloud, Wide Courtyard, etc.*) then the second (*Eternal God, Temporary World, Highest Heaven, etc.*) then the third (*Mortal Man, Heavy Weight, etc.*) mixed declensions will follow afterwards, (*Elegant Language, Lowly Hut; Deep Valley, High Tower, Easy Middle, Opportune Time, Spirit*)

<summary>This page provides pedagogical instructions on memorization and the systematic practice of Latin declensions by pairing nouns with adjectives. It also includes an editorial caution regarding the author's suggestion to lead with the native language rather than Latin.</summary>
<keywords>pedagogy, memorization, Latin grammar, declensions, noun-adjective agreement, linguistics, teaching methods</keywords>

--- Page 10 ---
<meta>Continuity: This page continues Comenius's instructions for teachers. Having just argued that Latin grammar should be simplified and made more natural for young students, he now addresses how to link Latin words with the students' native language and outlines the progression of memory exercises and noun-adjective pairings.</meta>

...**Invisible**, **Ripe Fruit**, **Necessary Thing**, etc. <note>original: "invisibilis, Fructus maturus, Res necessaria." These are examples of more complex pairings that do not follow the simplest patterns.</note>) which are to be set aside at the beginning.

3. Furthermore, let them practice <term>Declining: The process of changing the endings of nouns and adjectives to show their grammatical role (like the difference between "friend" and "friend's").</term> not by committing rules to memory, but through use. To this end, we have added a table below of the endings into which the **Cases** <note>The different forms a noun takes depending on its role in a sentence.</note> of the declensions fall; in the meantime, this chart will more than suffice in place of a formal Grammar book.

4. However, so that they better understand what is happening here, do not let the **Cases** be named by their technical titles (such as *Nominative*, *Genitive*, etc.), but instead let little questions be put before them:

1. What is this? Answer: **The Latin Language**. <note>original: "Lingua Latina" (Nominative case, used for the subject).</note>
2. Of which language is the word "**Come**"? <note>original: "Venite."</note> Answer: **Of the Latin Language**. <note>original: "Linguæ Latinæ" (Genitive case, showing possession).</note>
3. To what thing will you give your time? Answer: **To the Latin Language**. <note>original: "Linguæ Latinæ" (Dative case, showing the indirect object).</note>
4. What are you learning? Answer: **The Latin Language**. <note>original: "Linguam Latinam" (Accusative case, showing the direct object).</note>
5. Call out to it! Answer: **O Latin Language!** <note>original: "ô Lingua Latina" (Vocative case, used for direct address).</note>
6. In which language do you wish to speak? Answer: **In the Latin Language**. <note>original: "Linguâ Latinâ" (Ablative case, indicating the instrument or means).</note>

These things, I say, should be done this way from the beginning: (For once they have more easily grasped the use of these forms, they will learn the names of the Cases from a Grammar book later.) Moreover, let there be a more general formula that may serve for declining all Nouns, in both numbers <note>Singular and plural.</note>, and without the need for questions, as follows:

->**In the Singular:**<-

1. Behold, the **Blackboard**. <note>original: "Tabula Nigra."</note>
2. A part of the **Blackboard**. <note>original: "Tabulæ Nigræ."</note>
3. I add a part to the **Blackboard**. <note>original: "Tabulæ Nigræ."</note>
4. I see the **Blackboard**. <note>original: "Tabulam Nigram."</note>
5. O you **Blackboard**! <note>original: "ô tu Tabula Nigra."</note>
6. I see something on the **Blackboard**. <note>original: "in Tabulâ Nigrâ."</note>

<meta>Plural-</meta>

<summary>Comenius explains his "natural method" for teaching Latin grammar, advocating for students to learn noun cases through simple questions and practical use rather than memorizing abstract technical terms. He provides a step-by-step example of how to practice these variations using the example of a "Blackboard."</summary>
<keywords>Latin pedagogy, natural method, noun declension, grammatical cases, Blackboard, language instruction, Janua Linguarum</keywords>

--- Page 11 ---
<meta>Continuity: This page continues Comenius's pedagogical instructions for teaching Latin. The previous page demonstrated how to practice the cases of a singular noun ("The Latin Language") using conversational questions. This page concludes that exercise with the plural form and transitions into the method for teaching verbs and the importance of writing exercises.</meta>

### In the Plural: <note>original: "Pluraliter"</note>

1. Look, there are two **Blackboards**. <note>original: "Tabulæ Nigræ." In this context, a "black table" refers to the writing surface used in schools.</note>
2. Parts of two **Blackboards**.
3. I will add something to two **Blackboards**.
4. I see two **Blackboards**.
5. O, two **Blackboards**!
6. I see something on two **Blackboards**.

6. However, it will be good not only to <term>Decline: To list the different forms of a word based on its role in a sentence.</term> individual things aloud in this way (*by looking at the charts of the declensions*), but also to write them down.

Therefore, once a few little sentences have been committed to memory in the first hour, let the students write their exercises in notebooks prepared for that purpose in the following hour; namely, the declensions of those same things they have learned, in the manner already described. This will serve as a prelude to their future exercises in writing style.

V. In the same manner (in chapter II and those following), let the **Conjugations of Verbs** <term>Conjugation: The process of changing a verb's ending to show who is doing the action and when it is happening.</term> be practiced. A table for these is also added at the end of the *Vestibulum* <note>The "Vestibulum" (literally "The Porch") was Comenius's introductory textbook for children.</note>, so that in the meantime there is no need for any other summary of **Grammar**. But only the endings for the first form of the conjugations are provided: for it is enough that beginners have first learned these. We must carefully hide from students anything that might seem to threaten them with difficulty at first sight. And with that same intention, we have also concealed the <term>Supines: A specific type of verbal noun used in Latin to show purpose.</term>, and <term>Gerunds: A verb form that functions as a noun, such as "learning" or "writing."</term>, and even the **Infinitive forms of the past and future tenses**, because they deviate from the customs of the students' native language. There will be plenty of time to learn those later,

<summary>This page outlines the practical application of Comenius's method, moving from oral recitation of plural nouns to written exercises and the simplified introduction of verb conjugations.</summary>
<keywords>Latin pedagogy, declension, blackboard, writing exercises, verb conjugations, Vestibulum, teaching methods, grammar simplification</keywords>

--- Page 12 ---
<meta>Continuity: This page concludes Comenius's introductory instructions for the "Vestibulum" (The Porch), moving from the mechanical practice of word forms to the spiritual responsibility of the teacher. It transitions from the final steps of the introductory curriculum to the student's readiness for the next level of study, the "Janua" (The Gate).</meta>

...but a far more suitable opportunity will arise once they have entered the **Gate** <note>original: "Januam." Comenius refers to his more advanced textbook, *Janua Linguarum Reserata* (The Gate of Tongues Unlocked).</note>.

VI. Thus gradually (and as if doing something else entirely), the distinctions between *Nouns, Adjectives, Verbs, Adverbs, Prepositions, and Conjunctions*, and the general differences in their <term>Inflections: The way word endings change to show their role in a sentence.</term>, will become familiar. Specific details are to be reserved for the study of **Grammar**.

VII. Once they have completed the **Porch** <note>original: "Vestibulum," the introductory textbook for children.</note> in this manner, let them go through it again, reciting entire pages or leaves until they have it <term>perfectly: The original Latin "ad unguem" literally means "to the fingernail," a phrase used by ancient sculptors to describe a perfectly smooth finished surface.</term>. Then, and only then, may they be safely admitted to the **GATE**.

May God grant that we do not wander from the gate of Heaven, which is Christ, as we take up the duty of educating God’s little children who were born for Heaven. I beseech you, O Schoolmasters, to make this your primary concern for the sake of your own salvation and the salvation of those entrusted to you. Farewell. Given on the 4th of January,

->In the year 1633.<-

<vocab>The Gate, The Porch, Grammar, Schoolmasters, The gate of Heaven</vocab>

It ends with a solemn exhortation to educators, reminding them that teaching children is a sacred duty linked to their own salvation.</summary>

<meta>This page transitions from the book's introductory provenance notes to the actual pedagogical text. It is the beginning of a trilingual (Latin-German-Polish) vocabulary list, likely from a 17th-century edition of Jan Amos Comenius's educational works, specifically the "Vestibule" or "Gate" of languages. The layout provides the Latin root, followed by its German and Polish equivalents, ending with its grammatical category.</meta>

**Door** <note>original Latin: Janua</note>, the door <note>original German: die Thür</note> / the door <note>original Polish: drzwi</note> / Feminine, 1st Declension.
**Latinity** <note>original Latin: Latinitas</note>, the Latin language / the Latin language / Feminine, 3rd Declension.
**Vestibule** <note>original Latin: Vestibulum</note>, the courtyard / the entrance hall / Neuter, 2nd Declension.

**Greetings** <note>original Latin: Salve</note>, to be greeted / to be welcomed / Defective verb, 2nd conjugation.
**Boy** <note>original Latin: Puer</note>, the boy / the child / Masculine, 2nd Declension.
**I come** <note>original Latin: Venio</note>, to come / to arrive / Neuter verb, 4th conjugation.
**Little beginner** <note>original Latin: Tirunculus</note>, the little student / the young pupil / Masculine, 2nd Declension.
**I learn** <note>original Latin: Disco</note>, to learn / to study / Active verb, 3rd conjugation.
**Latin** <note>original Latin: Latinus</note>, that which is Latin / Latin / Adjective.
**Language** <note>original Latin: Lingua</note>, the language / the tongue / Feminine, 1st Declension.
**Beautiful** <note>original Latin: Pulcher</note>, the beautiful / the fair / Adjective.
**And** <note>original Latin: Et</note>, and / and / Copulative conjunction.
**Elegant** <note>original Latin: Elegans</note>, the handsome / the neat / Adjective.
**I grasp** <note>original Latin: Comprehendo</note>, to understand / to perceive / Active verb, 3rd conjugation.
**He, she, it** <note>original Latin: Is, ea, id</note>, this / that / the same / Pronominal adjective.
**For** <note>original Latin: Pro</note>, according to / according to / Preposition governing the ablative case.
**Your** <note>original Latin: Vester</note>, your [plural] / your / Pronominal adjective.
**Capacity** <note>original Latin: Captus</note>, the understanding / the mind / Masculine, 4th Declension.
**Various** <note>original Latin: Varius</note>, the manifold / the diverse / Adjective.
**Thing** <note>original Latin: Res</note>, the matter / the thing / Feminine, 5th Declension.
**Wisdom** <note>original Latin: Sapientia</note>, the wisdom / the wisdom / Feminine, 1st Declension.
**Seed** <note>original Latin: Semen</note>, the seed / the seed / Neuter, 3rd Declension.
**God** <note>original Latin: Deus</note>, the God / God / Masculine, 2nd Declension.
**You** <note>original Latin: Tu</note>, you [singular] / Substantive pronoun of all genders.
**I help** <note>original Latin: Juvo</note>, to help / to assist / Active verb, 1st conjugation.
**Teacher** <note>original Latin: Præceptor</note>, the schoolmaster / the instructor / Masculine, 3rd Declension.
**I love** <note>original Latin: Amo</note>, to love / to cherish / Active verb, 1st conjugation.

# THE GATE’S
## of Latinity
## the Vesti-
## bule.

<summary>This page marks the beginning of Jan Amos Comenius's "Vestibulum," an introductory Latin textbook. Written in three languages, it encourages young students to approach the study of Latin with diligence, promising that it will lead them to the seeds of wisdom and the favor of their teachers.</summary>

*Another, the other* <note>Latin: "Alius, a, ud." Translated from German "der andere" and Polish "drugi."</note>
*To praise / to set forth / active verb, 1st conjugation.* <note>Latin: "Laudo." German: "loben"; Polish: "wystawiać."</note>
*Self, the same / pronominal adjective.* <note>Latin: "Ipse, a, um." German: "dasselbe"; Polish: "sam."</note>
*To rejoice / neuter verb, 2nd conjugation.* <note>Latin: "Gaudeo" (transcribed as Gaudio). German: "sich frewen"; Polish: "radować się."</note>
*If / conditional conjunction.* <note>Latin: "Si." German: "so"; Polish: "jesli."</note>
*The beginning / neuter noun, 2nd declension.* <note>Latin: "Principium." German: "der Anfang"; Polish: "początek."</note>
*Difficult, heavy / adjective.* <note>Latin: "Difficilis, e." German: "das schwere"; Polish: "trudny."</note>
*The middle / neuter noun, 2nd declension.* <note>Latin: "Medium." German: "das Mittel"; Polish: "śrzodek."</note>
*To be / anomalous verb.* <note>Latin: "Sum, fui, esse." German: "seyn"; Polish: "być."</note>
*Easy, light / adjective.* <note>Latin: "Facilis, e." German: "das leichte"; Polish: "łatwi."</note>
*The end / masculine noun, 3rd declension.* <note>Latin: "Finis, is." German: "das Ende"; Polish: "koniec."</note>
*Pleasant, joyful / adjective.* <note>Latin: "Jucundus, a, um." German: "das lustige"; Polish: "przyjemny."</note>
*The chapter / the head / neuter noun, 2nd declension.* <note>Latin: "Caput, itis." German: "das Capittel"; Polish: "Rozdział."</note>
*The accident / chance occurrence / noun, 3rd declension.* <note>Latin: "Accidens, tis." German: "der Zufall"; Polish: "przypadek."</note>

The Sanctifier <note>Latin: *Sanctificátor*; German: *Heiligmacher*; Polish: *Poświeciciel*</note>, masculine noun of the 3rd declension.
**7** The World <note>Latin: *Mundus*; German: *Welt*; Polish: *świat*</note>, masculine noun of the 2nd declension.
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Temporal or worldly <note>Latin: *Temporarius*</note>, adjective of three terminations <note>This indicates the word has distinct endings for masculine, feminine, and neuter genders.</note>.
**8** Angel <note>Latin: *Angelus*; Polish: *Anjoł*</note>, masculine noun of the 2nd declension.
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Immortal <note>Latin: *Immortális*</note>, adjective of two terminations.
**9** Man or Human <note>Latin: *Homo*; German: *Mensch*; Polish: *człowiek*</note>, masculine noun of the 3rd declension.
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Mortal <note>Latin: *Mortális*</note>, adjective.
**10** The Body <note>Latin: *Corpus*; German: *Leib*; Polish: *ciało*</note>, neuter noun of the 3rd declension.
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Visible <note>Latin: *Visibilis*</note>, adjective.
**11** The Spirit <note>Latin: *Spiritus*; German: *Geist*; Polish: *Duch*</note>, masculine noun of the 4th declension.
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Invisible <note>Latin: *Invisibilis*</note>, adjective of two terminations.
**12** The Soul <note>Latin: *Anima*; German: *Seele*; Polish: *Dusza*</note>, feminine noun of the 1st declension.
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Likewise or in the same manner <note>Latin: *itidem*</note>, adverb of similarity.

&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Highest or Supreme <note>Latin: *Suprémus*</note>, adjective.

32 **Wood** <note>original: Lignum; German: das Holtz; Polish: Drewno</note>. **Neuter noun, 2nd declension.**
**Solid** <note>original: Solidus; German: dichte; Polish: dobrze zmocniony — well-strengthened</note>. **Adjective of 3 terminations.** <term>adj. 3. term.</term>

33 **Hole** <note>original: Forámen; German: das Loch; Polish: dziura</note>. **Neuter noun, 3rd declension.**
**Empty** <note>original: Vacuus; German: leere; Polish: prozny</note>. **Adjective of 3 terminations.**
**Void** <note>original: Inánis; German: ledige; Polish: czczy</note>. **Adjective of 2 terminations.**

34 **Chimney** <note>original: Caminus; German: der Camin; Polish: Komin</note>. **Masculine noun, 2nd declension.**
**Full** <note>original: Plenus; German: volle; Polish: pełny</note>. **Adjective of 3 terminations.** <term>adj. 3. term.</term>
**Smoke** <note>original: Fumus; German: der Rauch; Polish: dym</note>. **Masculine noun, 2nd declension.**

35 **Line** <note>original: Linea; German: die Linie (der ſtrich); Polish: linija</note>. **Feminine noun, 1st declension.**
**Straight** <note>original: Rectus; German: gerade; Polish: proſty</note>. **Adjective of 3 terminations.**
**Crooked** <note>original: Curvus; German: krum̃e; Polish: krzywy</note>. **Adjective of 3 terminations.**

36 **Surface** <note>original: Superficies; German: die außwendige breite — the outward breadth; Polish: powierz-chna ſerość</note>. **Feminine noun, 5th declension.**
**Rough** <note>original: Asper; German: rauche; Polish: chropowáty</note>. **Adjective of 3 terminations.** <term>adj. 3. term.</term>
**Smooth** <note>original: Lævis; German: glatte; Polish: gładki</note>. **Adjective of 2 terminations.**

37 **Weight** <note>original: Pondus; German: das Gewichte; Polish: wagá (gwicht)</note>. **Neuter noun, 3rd declension.**
**Heavy** <note>original: Gravis; German: ſchwere; Polish: cießki</note>. **Adjective of 2 terminations.**
**Light** <note>original: Levis; German: leichte; Polish: letki</note>. **Adjective of 2 terminations.**

38 **Number** <note>original: Numerus; German: die Zahl; Polish: liczba</note>. **Masculine noun, 2nd declension.**
**Even** <note>original: Par; German: gerade; Polish: rowny</note>. **Adjective of 1 termination.** <term>adj. un. ter.</term>
**Odd** <note>original: Impar; German: ungerade; Polish: nierowny</note>. **Adjective of 1 termination.** <term>A. un. tr.</term>

**39** **Sphere** <note>original: Globus</note>, the **ball** <note>original: die Kugel / kuluá</note>. <term>Masculine noun, 2nd declension</term>.
**Round** <note>original: Rotundus / rund / okrągły</note>. <term>Adjective, 3 endings (masculine, feminine, neuter)</term>.

**40** **Column** <note>original: Columna</note>, the **pillar** <note>original: die Seul / słup</note>. <term>Feminine noun, 1st declension</term>.
**Tapered** or **Cylindrical** <note>original: Teres / länglichrundte / wzdłuż okrągły</note>. <term>Adjective of one termination</term> <note>This describes a shape that is smooth, long, and rounded, like a well-turned pillar.</note>

**46** **Chalk** <note>original: Creta</note>, the **chalk** <note>original: die Kreyde / kretá</note>. <term>Feminine noun, 1st declension</term>.

<meta>This page continues the systematic vocabulary building from the previous section, moving from geometric shapes and primary colors to the heavens, physical properties of materials (transparency versus opacity), and the primary categories of taste (sweet, bitter, sour, and salty). This pedagogical structure follows the tradition of early modern "picture-word" books, associating nouns with their defining adjectives to help students build a descriptive Latin vocabulary through sensory experience.</meta>

51 **Firmament** <note>original: Firmamentum</note>, the **expanse of the sky** <note>original: die Feste deß Himmels / rospostrzeńie nieba</note>. <term>Neuter noun, 2nd declension</term>.

**Blue** <note>original: Cæruleus</note>, that which is blue or sky-colored <note>original: modry</note>. <term>Adjective, 3 endings (masculine, feminine, neuter)</term>.

52 **Parrot** <note>original: Psittaccus</note>, the parrot <note>original: pápugá</note>. <term>Masculine noun, 2nd declension</term>.

**Multicolored** <note>original: Versicolor</note>, that which is of many colors or variegated <note>original: pstry</note>. <term>Adjective of one termination (the same form for all genders)</term>.

53 **Glass** <note>original: Vitrum</note>, the glass <note>original: śkło</note>. <term>Neuter noun, 2nd declension</term>.

**Transparent** <note>original: Pellucidus</note>, that which is see-through or clear <note>original: przezroczysty</note>. <term>Adjective, 3 endings</term>.

54 **Board** <note>original: Asser</note>, the plank or wooden board <note>original: tarcica</note>. <term>Masculine noun, 3rd declension</term>.

**Opaque** <note>original: Opacus</note>, that which is shady or not see-through <note>original: ćieńisty</note>. <term>Adjective, 3 endings</term>.

55 **Water** <note>original: Aqua</note>, the water <note>original: wodá</note>. <term>Feminine noun, 1st declension</term>.

**Turbid** <note>original: Turbidus</note>, that which is cloudy or muddy <note>original: zmieszány</note>. <term>Adjective, 3 endings</term>.

**Clear** <note>original: Clarus</note>, that which is bright or pure <note>original: jásny</note>. <term>Adjective, 3 endings</term>.

**Flavor** <note>original: Sapor</note>, the taste or flavor <note>original: smák</note>. <term>Masculine noun, 3rd declension</term>.

56 **Honey** <note>original: Mel</note>, the honey <note>original: miod</note>. <term>Neuter noun, 3rd declension</term>.

**Sweet** <note>original: Dulcis</note>, that which is sweet <note>original: słodki</note>. <term>Adjective, 2 endings (masculine/feminine and neuter)</term>.

57 **Just as** <note>original: Sicut</note>, as or like. <term>Adverb of similarity</term>.

**Sugar** <note>original: Saccharum</note>, the sugar <note>original: cukier</note>. <term>Neuter noun, 2nd declension</term>.

58 **Gall** <note>original: Fel</note>, the bile or gall <note>original: zołć</note>. <term>Neuter noun, 3rd declension</term>.

**Bitter** <note>original: Amárus</note>, that which is bitter <note>original: gorzki</note>. <term>Adjective, 3 endings</term>.

59 **Vinegar** <note>original: Acétum</note>, the vinegar <note>original: Ocet</note>. <term>Neuter noun, 2nd declension</term>.

**Sour** <note>original: Acidus</note>, that which is sour or acidic <note>original: kwáśny</note>. <term>Adjective, 3 endings</term>.

60 **Salt** <note>original: Sal</note>, the salt <note>original: sol</note>. <term>Masculine and Neuter noun, 3rd declension</term>.

**Salty** <note>original: Salsus</note>, that which is salty or brined <note>original: słony</note>. <term>Adjective, 3 endings</term>.

<meta>This page continues the vocabulary-building exercise from the previous section, which focused on tastes like sweetness and bitterness. It moves into more complex sensory experiences, including smells (pleasant and foul) and physical properties of materials like wetness, dryness, hardness, and texture. The catchword "Pruna" on the previous page's metadata likely refers to "plums" or "coals," signaling a continuation of natural objects and their qualities.</meta>

61 **Pepper** <note>original: Piper, eris</note>, pepper <note>original: der Pfeffer / pieprz</note>. *Neuter noun, 3rd declension.*
**Sharp** <note>original: Acris, e</note>, sharp or pungent <note>original: der/die/das scharffe / ostry/ a/ e</note>. *Adjective with 2 endings.*

62 **Unripe** <note>original: Immaturus, a, um</note>, unseasonable or unripe <note>original: der die das unzeitige / niedoizrzáły a/ e</note>. *Adjective with 3 endings.* <term>technical term</term>
**Apple** <note>original: Pomum, i</note>, the apple <note>original: der Apffel / jabłko</note>. *Neuter noun, 2nd declension.*
**Harsh** <note>original: Acerbus, a, um</note>, bitter, harsh, or sour <note>original: der die das herbe / (raß) przykry / (cierpki) a/ e</note>. *Adjective with 3 endings.* <term>technical term</term>

63 **Scent** <note>original: Odor, óris</note>, smell, scent, or fragrance <note>original: der Geruch / wonia / wonność / zápach</note>. *Masculine noun, 3rd declension.*
**Sweet** <note>original: Suavis, e</note>, lovely or sweet-smelling <note>original: der die das liebliche / wdzięczny/ a/ e</note>. *Adjective with 2 endings.*

64 **Stench** <note>original: Fætor, óris</note>, a bad smell or stink <note>original: der Gestanck / smrod</note>. *Masculine noun, 3rd declension.*
**Foul** <note>original: Teter, tra, trum</note>, hideous or foul <note>original: der die das grewliche / sprosny/ a/ e</note>. *Adjective with 3 endings.* <term>technical term</term>
**Quality** <note>original: Qualitas, átis</note>, nature, condition, or property <note>original: die Beschaffenheit / jakość / (przyrodzenie)</note>. *Feminine noun, 3rd declension.*

65 **Mud** <note>original: Lutum, i</note>, dirt, mire, or mud <note>original: der Koht / błoto</note>. *Neuter noun, 2nd declension.*
**Moist** <note>original: Humidus, a, um</note>, damp or wet <note>original: der die das feuchte / wilgotny / a / e</note>. *Adjective with 3 endings.* <term>technical term</term>

66 **Dust** <note>original: Pulvis, eris</note>, dust or powder <note>original: der Staub / proch</note>. *Masculine noun, 3rd declension.*
**Dry** <note>original: Siccus, a, um</note>, dry or parched <note>original: der die das truckne / suchy/ a/ e</note>. *Adjective with 3 endings.*

67 **Bone** <note>original: Os, ossis</note>, the bone <note>original: das Bein / kość</note>. *Neuter noun, 3rd declension.*
**Hard** <note>original: Durus, a, um</note>, hard or tough <note>original: der die das harte / twardy/ a/ e</note>. *Adjective with 3 endings.*

68 **Flesh** <note>original: Caro, carnis</note>, meat or flesh <note>original: das Fleisch / mięso</note>. *Feminine noun, 3rd declension.*
**Soft** <note>original: Mollis, e</note>, soft or tender <note>original: der die das weiche / miękki/ a/ e</note>. *Adjective with 2 endings.*

69 **Marrow** <note>original: Medulla, æ</note>, the marrow or the crumb of bread <note>original: Krume / drzeń</note>. *Feminine noun, 1st declension.*
**Porous** <note>original: Porósus, a, um</note>, full of holes or loose-textured <note>original: der die das lockere / dziurkowaty/ ła/ łe</note>. *Adjective with 3 endings.* <term>technical term</term>

70 **Ice** <note>original: Glacies, éi</note>, the ice <note>original: das Eyß / lod</note>. *Feminine noun, 5th declension.*
**Slippery** <note>original: Lubricus, a, um</note>, slippery or slick <note>original: der die das schlipffrige / śliski / ka/ kie</note>. *Adjective with 3 endings.*

91 **Friendship** <note>original: *Amicitia*</note>, friendship <note>original: *die freundschafft*</note> / friendship <note>original: *przyiacielstwo*</note>. *Feminine, 1st declension.*
**Pleasant** <note>original: *Jucundus*</note>, the pleasant or delightful [one] / pleasant <note>original: *wdzieczny*</note>. *Adjective of three terminations.* <note>The "3" here refers to the three gender endings: -us, -a, -um.</note>

92 **Message** <note>original: *Nuncium*</note>, the message or news <note>original: *die Botſchafft*</note> / message <note>original: *poselstwo*</note>. *Neuter, 2nd declension.*
**Cheerful** <note>original: *Latus* (likely a misspelling of *laetus*)</note>, the cheerful or joyful / cheerful <note>original: *wesoły*</note>. *Adjective of three terminations.*
**Pleasing** <note>original: *Gratus*</note>, the dear or pleasing / pleasing <note>original: *wdzieczny*</note>. *Adjective of three terminations.*
**Welcome** <note>original: *Acceptus*</note>, the acceptable or pleasant / pleasant <note>original: *przyiemny*</note>. *Adjective of three terminations.*

93 **Time** <note>original: *Tempus*</note>, the time <note>original: *die Zeit*</note> / time <note>original: *czas*</note>. *Neuter, 3rd declension.*
**Opportune** <note>original: *Opportúnus*</note>, the fitting or timely / opportune <note>original: *sposobny*</note>. *Adjective of three terminations.*
**Inopportune** <note>original: *Importúnus*</note>, the inconvenient or untimely / inopportune <note>original: *niesposobny*</note>. *Adjective of three terminations.*

94 **Custom** <note>original: *Consuetúdo*</note>, the habit or custom <note>original: *die Gewonheit*</note> / custom <note>original: *zwyczaj*</note>. *Feminine, 3rd declension.*
**Good** <note>original: *Bonus*</note>, the good / good <note>original: *dobry*</note>. *Adjective of three terminations.*
**Perverse** <note>original: *Perverſus*</note>, the perverse or twisted / perverse <note>original: *przewrotny*</note>. *Adjective of three terminations.*

95 **Convenient** <note>original: *Commodus*</note>, the convenient or comfortable / fitting <note>original: *sposobny*</note>.
**Inconvenient** <note>original: *Incommodus*</note>, the inconvenient / unfitting <note>original: *niesposobny*</note>. *Adjective of three terminations.*

96 **Instrument** <note>original: *Inſtrumentum*</note>, the tool <note>original: *das Werckzeug*</note> / vessel or tool <note>original: *naczynie*</note>. *Neuter, 2nd declension.*
**Apt** <note>original: *Aptus*</note>, the capable or fit / fit <note>original: *sposobny*</note>. *Adjective of three terminations.*
**Inept** <note>original: *Ineptus*</note>, the incapable or unfit / unfit <note>original: *niesposobny*</note>. *Adjective of three terminations.*

97 **Surgeon** <note>original: *Chirurgus*</note>, the wound-doctor <note>original: *der Wundarßt*</note> / barber-surgeon <note>original: *barwierż*</note>. *Masculine, 2nd declension.* <note>In the early modern period, surgeons were often part of the barbers' guild, performing minor surgeries and bloodletting.</note>
**Suitable** <note>original: *Idoneus*</note>, the skillful or suitable / worthy <note>original: *godny*</note>. *Adjective of three terminations.*
**Unsuitable** <note>original: *Inidoneus*</note>, the unskillful or unsuitable / unworthy <note>original: *niegodny*</note>.

98 **Matter** <note>original: *Res*</note>, the thing or matter <note>original: *die Sache*</note> / thing <note>original: *rzecz*</note>. *Feminine, 5th declension.*
**Necessary** <note>original: *Neceſſari⁹*</note>, the necessary / necessary <note>original: *potrzebny*</note>. <note>The superscript "9" is a common Latin abbreviation for the suffix "-us."</note>
**Superfluous** <note>original: *ſupervacaneus*</note>, the unnecessary / excessive or superfluous <note>original: *zbytny*</note>. *Adjective of three terminations.*

99 Opinion <note>original Latin: *opinio, -onis*</note>, the opinion <note>original German: *die Meinung*</note> / the belief <note>original Polish: *mniemánie*</note>. Feminine, 3rd declension.
True <note>original Latin: *verus, -a, -um*</note>, the true <note>original German: *wahre*</note> / the true <note>original Polish: *prawdziwy*</note>. Adjective, 3 endings.
False <note>original Latin: *falsus, -a, -um*</note>, the false <note>original German: *falsche*</note> / the false <note>original Polish: *fałßywy*</note>. Adjective, 3 endings.

100 Use <note>original Latin: *usus, -ûs*</note>, the benefit <note>original German: *der Nuß*</note> / the profit <note>original Polish: *pożytek*</note>. Masculine, 4th declension.
Useful <note>original Latin: *utilis, -e*</note>, the useful <note>original German: *das nüßliche*</note> / the beneficial <note>original Polish: *pożyteczny*</note>. Adjective, 2 endings.
Harmful <note>original Latin: *noxius, -a, -um*</note>, the harmful <note>original German: *schädliche*</note> / the injurious <note>original Polish: *skodliwy*</note>. Adjective, 3 endings.
Similar <note>original Latin: *similis, -e*</note>, the same <note>original German: *gleiche*</note> / the similar <note>original Polish: *podobny*</note>. Adjective, 2 endings.
Dissimilar <note>original Latin: *dissimilis, -e*</note>, the unequal <note>original German: *ungleiche*</note> / the unlike <note>original Polish: *niepodobny*</note>. Adjective, 2 endings <term>term: "term." here refers to the grammatical termination or ending of the word</term>.

102 Forest <note>original Latin: *sylva, -æ*</note>, the woods <note>original German: *der Wald*</note> / the forest <note>original Polish: *las*</note>. Feminine, 1st declension.
Dense <note>original Latin: *densus, -a, -um*</note>, the thick <note>original German: *dicke*</note> / the dense <note>original Polish: *gęsty*</note>. Adjective, 3 endings.
Sparse <note>original Latin: *rarus, -a, -um*</note>, the thin <note>original German: *dünne*</note> / the rare or thin <note>original Polish: *rzadki*</note>. Adjective, 3 endings.

103 Field <note>original Latin: *ager, gri*</note>, the acre <note>original German: *der Acker*</note> / the field <note>original Polish: *rola*</note>. Masculine, 2nd declension. <note>The note "Adj. 3" likely refers to the following adjectives.</note>
Fertile <note>original Latin: *fæcūdus, -a, -um*</note>, the fruitful <note>original German: *fruchtbare*</note> / the fertile <note>original Polish: *płodny*</note>.

Slow <note>original Latin: *tardus, -a, -um*</note>, the slow <note>original German: *langsame*</note> / the lazy <note>original Polish: *leniwy*</note>. Adjective, 3 endings.

105 Runner <note>original Latin: *cursor, -óris*</note>, the runner <note>original German: *der Läuffer*</note> / the messenger <note>original Polish: *posel*</note>. Masculine, 3rd declension.
Brisk <note>original Latin: *alacer, -e*</note>, the lively <note>original German: *hurtige*</note> / the eager <note>original Polish: *ochotny*</note>. Adjective, 2 endings.

106 Worker <note>original Latin: *operarius, -i*</note>, the worker <note>original German: *der Arbeiter*</note> / the laborer <note>original Polish: *robotnik*</note>. Masculine, 2nd declension.

The layout remains a comparative trilingual table, which was a common pedagogical tool in early modern Europe for teaching Latin alongside vernacular languages. The catchword at the bottom, "Cibus," signals that the following page will transition into the vocabulary of food and sustenance.</meta>

--- Page 38 ---
<meta>This page continues the numbered vocabulary list, picking up from the catchword "Cibus" (Food) on the previous page. The text remains a pedagogical tool, likely from a 17th-century nomenclator, providing Latin terms alongside their German and Polish equivalents. This specific section moves from the physical world of food and flavor to the intellectual world of speech, history, and the degrees of scholarly achievement.</meta>

108. **Food,** <note>original: "Cibus, i"</note> the meal or nourishment <note>German: "die Speise"; Polish: "potrawa (pokarm)"</note>. Masculine noun of the 2nd declension.
*Raw,* <note>original: "Crudus, a, um"</note> that which is uncooked or raw <note>German: "der die das rohe"; Polish: "surowy/wa/we"</note>.
*Cooked,* <note>original: "Coctus, a, um"</note> that which is boiled or prepared <note>German: "der die das gekochte"; Polish: "uwarzony/na/ne"</note>. Adjective with three endings.

109. **Of wild game,** <note>original: "Ferinus, a, um"</note> of a wild nature or belonging to wild animals <note>German: "wilder art"; Polish: "dziki/ka/kie"</note>. Adjective with three endings.
*Savory,* <note>original: "Sapidus, a, um"</note> that which is flavorful or tasty <note>German: "der die das schmackhaffte"; Polish: "smáczny/na/ne"</note>. Adjective with three endings.
*Tasteless,* <note>original: "Insipidus, a, um"</note> that which is unsavory or bland <note>German: "der die das unschmackhaffte"; Polish: "niesmáczny/na/ne"</note>. Adjective with three endings.

110. **Speech,** <note>original: "Sermo, ónis"</note> a discourse or talk <note>German: "die Rede"; Polish: "mowa"</note>. Masculine noun of the 3rd declension.
*Humorous,* <note>original: "Jocosus, a, um"</note> that which is jesting, playful, or humorous <note>German: "der die das scherzhaffte"; Polish: "zartobliwy/wa/we"</note>. Adjective with three endings.
*Serious,* <note>original: "Serius, a, um"</note> earnest or grave <note>German: "ernsthaffte"; Polish: "powazny/na/ne"</note>.

111. **History,** <note>original: "Historia, æ"</note> a story, account, or chronicle <note>German: "die Geschichte"; Polish: "historia (księga dzieiow)" – literally "a book of deeds."</note>. Feminine noun of the 1st declension.
*Certain,* <note>original: "Certus, a, um"</note> sure, fixed, or true <note>German: "gewisse"; Polish: "prawdziwy/wa/we"</note>.
*Doubtful,* <note>original: "Dubius, a, um"</note> that which is uncertain or wavering <note>German: "der die das zweiffelhaffte"; Polish: "wątpliwy/wa/we"</note>. Adjective with three endings.

112. **Testimony,** <note>original: "Teſtimonium, i"</note> a witness, evidence, or testimony <note>German: "das Zeugniß"; Polish: "swiadectwo"</note>. Neuter noun of the 2nd declension.
*True,* <note>original: "Verus, a, um"</note> truthful or certain <note>German: "warhaffte"; Polish: "pewny/na/ne"</note>. Adjective with three endings.
*False,* <note>original: "Falſus, a, um"</note> that which is deceptive or untrue <note>German: "der die das falsche"; Polish: "falzywy/wa/we"</note>. Adjective with three endings.

---

113. **First,** <note>original: "Primus, a, um"</note> the first <note>German: "erste"; Polish: "pierwszy/sza/sze"</note>. Adjective with three endings.
*Learned,* <note>original: "Doctus, a, um"</note> educated or scholarly <note>German: "gelehrte"; Polish: "uczony/na/ne"</note>. Adjective with three endings.

114. **Second,** <note>original: "Secundus, a, um"</note> the other or second <note>German: "der die das andere"; Polish: "drugi/ga/ge (wtory)"</note>. Adjective with three endings.
*More learned,* <note>original: "Doctior, us"</note> more scholarly or better educated <note>German: "gelehrtere"; Polish: "uczeńszy/sza/sze"</note>. Adjective with two endings <note>This is the comparative form of the adjective.</note>.

115. **Third,** <note>original: "Tertius, a, um"</note> the third <note>German: "dritte"; Polish: "trzeci/cia/cie"</note>. Adjective with three endings.
*Most learned,* <note>original: "Doctiſſimus, a, um"</note> the most scholarly or most highly educated <note>German: "der die das gelehrteste"; Polish: "nauczeńszy/nsza/nsze"</note>. Adjective with three endings <note>This is the superlative form of the adjective.</note>.

178 **The Element** <note>original Latin: *Elementum*; German: *das Element*; Polish: *żywioł*</note>. *Neuter, 2nd declension.*
**I nourish** <note>original Latin: *Alo*; German: *nehren*; Polish: *żywie* or *karmie*</note>, *I have nourished, nourished or fed, to nourish*. 3rd *Active conjugation.*
**I** <note>original Latin: *Ego*; German: *ich*; Polish: *ja*</note>. *Substantive pronoun.*

149 **The Fire** <note>original Latin: *Ignis*; German: *das Fewer*; Polish: *ogień*</note>. *Masculine, 3rd declension.*
**I burn** <note>original Latin: *Ardeo*; German: *brennen*; Polish: *gore*</note>, *I burned, burned, to burn*. 2nd *Neuter conjugation.* <note>In this context, "neuter" refers to an intransitive verb—an action that doesn't take a direct object.</note>

150 **The Flame** <note>original Latin: *Flamma*; German: *die Flamme*; Polish: *płomień*</note>. *Feminine, 1st declension.*
**I blaze** <note>original Latin: *Flagro*; German: *lodern*; Polish: *pałam*</note>, *I blazed, blazed, to blaze*. 1st *Neuter conjugation.*
**I smoke** <note>original Latin: *Fumo*; German: *rauchen*; Polish: *kurzy sie*</note>, *I smoked, smoked, to smoke*. 1st *Neuter conjugation.*

151 **The Spark** <note>original Latin: *Scintilla*; German: *der Funck*; Polish: *iskra*</note>. *Feminine, 1st declension.*
**I glow** <note>original Latin: *Glisco*; German: *glimmen*; Polish: *tli* or *sili sie*</note>, *to glow or flare up*. *Neuter, 3rd conjugation.*
**The Ash** <note>original Latin: *Cinis*; German: *die Asche*; Polish: *popioł*</note>. *Masculine, 3rd declension.*

152 **The Thunder** <note>original Latin: *Tonitru*; German: *der Donner*; Polish: *piorun*</note>. *Neuter, 4th declension.*
**I thunder** <note>original Latin: *Tono*; German: *donnern*; Polish: *grzmi*</note>, *I thundered, thundered, to thunder*. 1st *Neuter conjugation.*

153 **The Lightning** <note>original Latin: *Fulgur*; German: *der Blitz*; Polish: *błyskawica*</note>. *Neuter, 3rd declension.*
**It lightens** <note>original Latin: *Fulguro*; German: *wetterleuchten*; Polish: *błyska sie*</note>, *it flashed, flashed, to flash*. 1st *Neuter conjugation.*

154 **The Wind** <note>original Latin: *Ventus*; German: *der Wind*; Polish: *wiatr*</note>. *Masculine, 2nd declension.*
**I blow** <note>original Latin: *Flo*; German: *wehen*; Polish: *wieje*</note>, *I blew, blown, to blow*. 2nd *Neuter conjugation.*

<insert>Library of Saint Genevieve, Paris</insert> <note>This handwritten insertion identifies the book as formerly belonging to the collection of the Abbey of Saint Genevieve in Paris.</note>

**Vocabulary Reference:**
<term>Elements</term> (Latin: *Elementa*): In the historical context of this book, these refer to the four classical elements (fire, air, water, earth) believed to be the building blocks of all matter.
<term>Fire</term> (Latin: *Ignis*): Used here to introduce associated phenomena like flames, sparks, and smoke.
<term>Thunder and Lightning</term> (Latin: *Tonitru* and *Fulgur*): These atmospheric events were grouped with the element of Fire or Air in early scientific classifications.

<meta>This page continues the thematic exploration of the physical world. Following the previous page's introduction to the four elements, it focuses on water in its various forms—meteorological (rain, snow, hail) and terrestrial (springs, wells). The vocabulary is organized to show cause and effect: rain "falls" with "force," and hail "harms" the "ear of grain." The catchword at the bottom, *Vapor*, indicates the next page will likely discuss mist, steam, or heat.</meta>

155 **Rain** <note>original: Latin *Pluvia*, German *der Regen*, Polish *deʃzcz*</note> [Feminine noun, 1st declension]
**To rain** <note>original: Latin *Pluo*, German *regnen*, Polish *deʃzcz pada* (the rain falls)</note> [3rd conjugation, Neuter/Intransitive verb]

156 **Downpour** <note>original: Latin *Imber*, German *der Plaßregen* (heavy rain), Polish *deʃzcz wielki* (great rain) or *gwałtowny* (violent rain)</note> [Masculine noun, 3rd declension]
**To fall** <note>original: Latin *Labor*, German *fallen*, Polish *upádam*</note> [3rd conjugation, Deponent verb]
**With** <note>original: Latin *Cum*, German *mit*, Polish *z*</note> [Preposition governing the ablative case]
**Force** <note>original: Latin *Impetus*, German *das Ungeʃtüm* (vehemence), Polish *popedliwość* (rashness) or *gwałt* (violence)</note> [Masculine noun, 4th declension]

157 **Snow** <note>original: Latin *Nix*, German *der Schnee*, Polish *śnieg*</note> [Feminine noun, 3rd declension]
**To snow** <note>original: Latin *Ningo*, German *ʃchneyen*, Polish *śnieg páda* (the snow falls)</note> [3rd conjugation, Neuter/Intransitive verb]

158 **Hail** <note>original: Latin *Grando*, German *der Hagel*, Polish *grad*</note> [Feminine noun, 3rd declension]
**To harm** <note>original: Latin *Noceo*, German *ʃchaden*, Polish *ʃkodze*</note> [2nd conjugation, Neuter/Intransitive verb]
**Ear of grain** <note>original: Latin *Ariʃta*, German *die Kornähr*, Polish *kłos*</note> [Feminine noun, 1st declension]

159 **Water** <note>original: Latin *Aqua*, German *das Waʃʃer*, Polish *woda*</note> [Feminine noun, 1st declension]
**Level** <note>original: Latin *Planus*, German *ebene*, Polish *rowny*</note> [Adjective]
**To flow** <note>original: Latin *Fluo*, German *flieʃʃen*, Polish *ptyne* (I flow) or *cieke* (I leak/run)</note> [3rd conjugation, Neuter/Intransitive verb]

160 **From** <note>original: Latin *Ex*, German *aus*, Polish *z*</note> [Preposition governing the ablative case]
**Vessel** <note>original: Latin *Vas*, German *das Faß*, Polish *naczynie* (container/dish)</note> [Neuter noun, 3rd declension]
**To flow or drip** <note>original: Latin *Mano*, German *rinnen*, Polish *cieke* or *ptyne*</note> [1st conjugation, Neuter/Intransitive verb]

161 **Spring** <note>original: Latin *Fons*, German *der Springbrunn*, Polish *zrzodło* (source)</note> [Masculine noun, 3rd declension]
**To leap out** <note>original: Latin *Salio*, German *ʃpringen*, Polish *ʃkacze* (I jump) or *wyʃkakuje* (I leap out)</note> [4th conjugation, Neuter/Intransitive verb]

162 **Well** <note>original: Latin *Puteus*, German *der Brunn*, Polish *ʃtudnia*</note> [Masculine noun, 2nd declension]
**To draw water** <note>original: Latin *Haurio*, German *ʃchöpffen*, Polish *czerpam*</note> [4th conjugation, Active/Transitive verb]

163 **Drop** <note>original: Latin *Gutta*, German *der Tropff*, Polish *kropla*</note> [Feminine noun, 1st declension]
**To trickle** <note>original: Latin *Deʃtillo*, German *träuffen*, Polish *ściekam* or *oʃciekam* (to drain off)</note> [1st conjugation, Neuter/Intransitive verb]

164 **Mist**, of the mist <note>original Latin: *Vapor, óris*</note>; the mist <note>original German: *der Dunst*</note>; steam <note>original Polish: *pará*</note>. <term>Masculine noun, 3rd declension</term>.
**Dew**, of the dew <note>original Latin: *Ros, roris*</note>; the dew <note>original German: *der Taw*</note>; dew <note>original Polish: *rosá*</note>. <term>Masculine noun, 3rd declension</term>.
**Hoarfrost**, of the frost <note>original Latin: *Pruina, æ*</note>; the frost <note>original German: *der Reiff*</note>; frost <note>original Polish: *szron*</note>. <term>Feminine noun, 1st declension</term>.

**Plant** or **Sprout**, of the sprout <note>original Latin: *Planta, æ*</note>; the growth or plant <note>original German: *das Gewächs*</note>; sprout or shoot <note>original Polish: *látorosl*</note>. <term>Feminine noun, 1st declension</term>.

165 **Herb** or **Plant**, of the herb <note>original Latin: *Herba, æ*</note>; the herb or plant <note>original German: *das Kraut*</note>; herb or weed <note>original Polish: *ziele*</note>. <term>Feminine noun, 1st declension</term>.
**I grow**, I have grown, grown, to grow <note>original Latin: *Cresco, crevi, cretum, scere*</note>; to grow <note>original German: *wachsen*</note>; I grow <note>original Polish: *roste*</note>. <term>3rd conjugation, Neuter (intransitive)</term>.
**I decrease**, I have decreased, decreased, to decrease <note>original Latin: *Decresco, decrévi, decrétum, scere*</note>; to decrease <note>original German: *abnehmen*</note>; I decrease or ebb <note>original Polish: *ubywam*</note>. <term>3rd conjugation, Neuter (intransitive)</term>.

166 **Leaf**, of the leaf <note>original Latin: *Folium, i*</note>; the leaf <note>original German: *das Blat*</note>; leaf <note>original Polish: *list*</note>. <term>Neuter noun, 2nd declension</term>.
**I am green**, I was green, to be green <note>original Latin: *Vireo, ui, ére*</note>; to be green or turn green <note>original German: *grünen*</note>; I turn green <note>original Polish: *zielenie sie*</note>. <term>2nd conjugation, Neuter (intransitive)</term>.

167 **Flower**, of the flower <note>original Latin: *Flos, oris*</note>; the flower <note>original German: *die Blume*</note>; flower <note>original Polish: *kwiat*</note>. <term>Masculine noun, 3rd declension</term>.
**I bloom**, I have bloomed, to bloom <note>original Latin: *Floreo, ui, ére*</note>; to bloom <note>original German: *blühen*</note>; I bloom <note>original Polish: *kwitne*</note>. <term>2nd conjugation, Neuter (intransitive)</term>.

168 **Fruit**, of the fruit <note>original Latin: *Fructus, ûs*</note>; the fruit <note>original German: *die Frucht*</note>; fruit <note>original Polish: *owoc*</note>. <term>Masculine noun, 4th declension</term>.
**Tree**, of the tree <note>original Latin: *Arbor, oris*</note>; the tree <note>original German: *der Baum*</note>; tree <note>original Polish: *drzewo*</note>. <term>Feminine noun, 3rd declension</term>.
**I ripen**, I have ripened, to ripen <note>original Latin: *Maturesco, maturui, maturescere*</note>; to ripen <note>original German: *reiffen*</note>; it ripens <note>original Polish: *zrzeie*</note>. <term>3rd conjugation, Neuter (intransitive)</term>.

169 **Who** or **Which** <note>original Latin: *Qui, quæ, quod*</note>; which [masculine, feminine, or neuter] <note>original German: *welcher/ welche/ welches*</note>; which [masculine, feminine, or neuter] <note>original Polish: *ktory/ ra/ re*</note>. <term>Adjectival pronoun</term>.
**I pluck**, I have plucked, plucked, to pluck <note>original Latin: *Carpo, carpsi, carptum, carpere*</note>; to break off <note>original German: *abbrechen*</note>; I tear off or pluck <note>original Polish: *urywam*</note>. <term>3rd conjugation, Active (transitive)</term>.

170 **I bend**, I have bent, bent, to bend <note>original Latin: *Flecto, flexi, flexum, flectere*</note>; to bend <note>original German: *beugen*</note>; I bend <note>original Polish: *nagi nam*</note>. <term>3rd conjugation, Active (transitive)</term>.
**Branch**, of the branch <note>original Latin: *Ramus, i*</note>; the branch or bough <note>original German: *der Ast*</note>; branch <note>original Polish: *gałąź*</note>. <term>Masculine noun, 2nd declension</term>.

The worm <note>original Latin: *vermis, is*; German: *der Wurm*; Polish: *robak*</note> [Masculine, 3rd declension].

201 The deer or stag <note>original Latin: *cervus, i*; German: *der Hirsch*; Polish: *jeleń* (transcribed as *teleń*)</note> [Masculine, 2nd declension].
I run <note>original Latin: *curro, cucurri, cursum, rere*; German: *lauffen*; Polish: *bieżę* or *biegam*</note> [3rd conjugation, Intransitive].

202 The horse <note>original Latin: *equus, i*; German: *das Pferd*; Polish: *koń*</note> [Masculine, 2nd declension].
I neigh <note>original Latin: *hinnio, ivi, itum, ire*; German: *weihern*; Polish: *rżę*</note> [4th conjugation, Intransitive].

203 The ox <note>original Latin: *bos, bovis*; German: *der Ochß*; Polish: *woł*</note> [Common gender, 3rd declension].
I bellow or low <note>original Latin: *mugio, ivi, itum, ire*; German: *brüllen*; Polish: *ryczę* or *beczę*</note> [4th conjugation, Intransitive].

204 The lamb <note>original Latin: *agnus, i*; German: *das Lamb*; Polish: *baránek*</note> [Masculine, 2nd declension].
I bleat <note>original Latin: *balo, ávi, átum, áre*; German: *bläcken*; Polish: *beczę*</note> [1st conjugation, Intransitive].

205 The pig <note>original Latin: *porcus, i*; German: *das Schwein*; Polish: *wieprz*</note> [Masculine, 2nd declension].
The sow or pig <note>original Latin: *sus, suis*; German: *die Saw*; Polish: *świnia*</note> [Common gender, 3rd declension].
I grunt <note>original Latin: *grunnio* (transcribed as *grummo*), *ivi, itum, ire*; German: *grunzen*; Polish: *kwiczę*</note> [4th conjugation, Intransitive].

206 The bear <note>original Latin: *ursus, i*; German: *der Bär*; Polish: *niedźwiedź*</note> [Masculine, 2nd declension].
I growl or mumble <note>original Latin: *murmuro, ávi, átum, áre*; German: *brummen*; Polish: *mruczę*</note> [1st conjugation, Intransitive].

207 The wolf <note>original Latin: *lupus, i*; German: *der Wolff*; Polish: *wilk*</note> [Masculine, 2nd declension].
I howl <note>original Latin: *ululo, ávi, átum, áre*; German: *heulen*; Polish: *wyję*</note> [1st conjugation, Intransitive].

208 The dog <note>original Latin: *canis, is*; German: *der Hund*; Polish: *pies*</note> [Common gender, 3rd declension].
I bark <note>original Latin: *latro, ávi, átum, áre*; German: *bellen*; Polish: *szczekam*</note> [1st conjugation, Intransitive].

209 The ram <note>original Latin: *aries, etis*; German: *der Widder*; Polish: *barán*</note> [Masculine, 3rd declension].
I butt or strike <note>original Latin: *arieto, ávi, átum, áre*; German: *stossen*; Polish: *trykam*</note> [1st conjugation, Active].

210 The wild beast <note>original Latin: *bestia, æ*; German: *das wilde Thier*; Polish: *zwierz* or *zwierzę*</note> [Feminine, 1st declension].
I tear to pieces <note>original Latin: *lanio, ávi, átum, áre*; German: *zerreißen*; Polish: *szárpam* or *roździeram*</note> [1st conjugation, Active].

<meta>catchword: Boar (Aper)</meta>

<margin>218</margin> **Woodworm** or **maggot** <note>original: Latin *termes*; German *die Made*; Polish *czerw / drzewny* (wood-worm)</note>, masculine, 3rd declension.
**Fat** or **suet** <note>original: Latin *adeps*; German *das Schmer*; Polish *sadło*</note>, common gender, 3rd declension.
**Bacon** or **lard** <note>original: Latin *lardum*; German *der Speck*; Polish *słonina*</note>, neuter, 2nd declension.
**I spoil, ruin,** or **corrupt** <note>original: Latin *corrumpo*; German *verderben*; Polish *psuie*</note>, 3rd conjugation, active.

<margin>219</margin> **Louse** <note>original: Latin *pediculus*; German *die Lauß*; Polish *wés*</note>, masculine, 2nd declension.
**I bite** <note>original: Latin *mordeo*; German *beissen*; Polish *kąsam*</note>, 2nd conjugation, active.

**Skin** <note>original: Latin *cutis*; German *die Haut*; Polish *skora*</note>, feminine, 3rd declension.
<margin>220</margin> **Flea** <note>original: Latin *pulex*; German *der Floh*; Polish *pchłá*</note>, masculine, 3rd declension.
**Bedbug** <note>original: Latin *cimex*; German *die Wanze*; Polish *pluskwá*</note>, masculine, 3rd declension.
**Gnat** or **mosquito** <note>original: Latin *culex*; German *die Mücke*; Polish *komor*</note>, common gender, 3rd declension.

<margin>221</margin> **Ant** <note>original: Latin *formica*; German *die Ameiß*; Polish *mrowka*</note>, feminine, 1st declension.
**Laborious** or **hard-working** <note>original: Latin *laboriosus*; German *arbeitsam*; Polish *pracowity*</note>, adjective of the 3rd declension type <note>In early modern textbooks, adjectives were often grouped by their ending patterns rather than just declension numbers.</note>.

<margin>222</margin> **Spider** <note>original: Latin *aranea*; German *die Spinne*; Polish *paiąk*</note>, feminine, 1st declension.
**Skillful, artistic,** or **ingenious** <note>original: Latin *artificiosus*; German *kunstreich*; Polish *subtelny / stuczny*</note>, adjective.

<margin>223</margin> **Bee** <note>original: Latin *apis*; German *die Biene*; Polish *pszczoła*</note>, feminine, 3rd declension.
**I sting** or **prick** <note>original: Latin *pungo*; German *stechen*; Polish *kole / kąsam*</note>, 3rd conjugation, active.
**Stinger** <note>original: Latin *aculeus*; German *der Stachel*; Polish *żądło*</note>, masculine, 2nd declension.

<margin>224</margin> **I live** <note>original: Latin *vivo*; German *leben*; Polish *zyie*</note>, 3rd conjugation, neuter.
**I flourish, thrive,** or **am vigorous** <note>original: Latin *vigeo*; German *grünen* (to be green/vigorous); Polish *mocnieie / trwam / żyie* (I grow strong/endure/live)</note>, 2nd conjugation, neuter.

<margin>225</margin> **I do, act,** or **perform** <note>original: Latin *ago*; German *thun*; Polish *czynie / sprawuie*</note>, 3rd conjugation, active.
**Various** or **manifold** <note>original: Latin *varius*; German *mancherley*; Polish *rozmaity*</note>, adjective.
**Body** <note>original: Latin *corpus*; German *der Leib*; Polish *ciało*</note>, neuter, 2nd declension <note>Though usually 3rd declension in standard Latin, it is marked here as 2nd, possibly reflecting a specific pedagogical grouping.</note>.
**Mind, spirit,** or **soul** <note>original: Latin *animus*; German *das Gemüht*; Polish *Umysł*</note>, masculine, 2nd declension.

**226** The **Head** <note>original Latin: *caput*; German: *das Haupt*; Polish: *głowa*</note>. [Neuter, 3rd declension]
I **fill up** <note>original Latin: *repleo*; German: *erfüllen*; Polish: *napełniam*</note>. [2nd conjugation, Active]
The **brain** <note>original Latin: *cerebrum*; German: *das Gehirn*; Polish: *mozg*</note>. [Neuter, 2nd declension]

**227** I **sneeze** <note>original Latin: *sternuto*; German: *niesen*; Polish: *kicham*</note>. [1st conjugation, Neuter verb]
I **cleanse** or **purify** <note>original Latin: *purgo*; German: *reinigen*; Polish: *oczyszczam* or *chędzę* (an archaic Polish term for cleaning)</note>. [1st conjugation, Active]

**228** I **cover** <note>original Latin: *tego*; German: *bedecken*; Polish: *zakrywam* or *pokrywam*</note>. [3rd conjugation, Active]
The **hair** <note>original Latin: *capillus*; German: *das Haar*; Polish: *włos*</note>. [Masculine, 2nd declension]

**229** I **except** or **exclude** <note>original Latin: *excipio*; German: *ausschließen*; Polish: *wyjmuję* or *wyzwalam*</note>. [3rd conjugation, Active]
The **face** or **countenance** <note>original Latin: *vultus*; German: *das Angesicht*; Polish: *twarz*</note>. [Masculine, 4th declension]

**230** **Sometimes** <note>original Latin: *quandoque*; German: *bisweilen*; Polish: *podczas*</note>. [Adverb of time]
**Nevertheless** or **yet** <note>original Latin: *tamen*; German: *doch*; Polish: *też*</note>. [Adversative conjunction]
**Bald** <note>original Latin: *calvus*; German: *kahl*; Polish: *łysy*</note>. [Adjective, 3rd declension]

**231** **None** or **no one** <note>original Latin: *nullus*; German: *keiner*; Polish: *żaden* or *nikt*</note>. [Adjective, 3rd declension]
A **limb** or **member** <note>original Latin: *membrum*; German: *das Glied*; Polish: *członek*</note>. [Neuter, 2nd declension]
**In vain** <note>original Latin: *frustra*; German: *vergebens*; Polish: *darmo*</note>. [Adverb]
I **give** <note>original Latin: *do*; German: *geben*; Polish: *daję*</note>. [1st conjugation, Active]

**232** An **eye** <note>original Latin: *oculus*; German: *das Auge*; Polish: *oko*</note>. [Masculine, 2nd declension]
I **see** or **discern** <note>original Latin: *cerno*; German: *sehen*; Polish: *widzę* or *patrzam*</note>. [3rd conjugation, Active]

**233** **Through** <note>original Latin: *per*; German: *durch*; Polish: *przez*</note>. [Preposition governing the accusative case]
The **nose** <note>original Latin: *nasus*; German: *die Nase*; Polish: *nos*</note>. [Masculine, 2nd declension]
I **discharge** or **empty** <note>original Latin: *excerno*; German: *auswerfen*; Polish: *wyczyszczam* or *wyprożniać się*</note>. [3rd conjugation, Active]

**234** The **forehead** <note>original Latin: *frons*; German: *die Stirn*; Polish: *czoło*</note>. [Feminine, 3rd declension]
I **have** <note>original Latin: *habeo*; German: *haben*; Polish: *mam*</note>. [2nd conjugation, Active]

235 **Cheek**, the cheek / <note>original: *die Wange* (German) / *policzek* (Polish)</note> / berry on the face <note>original Polish: *jágodá ná twarzy*, an archaic and poetic way to describe the prominence of the cheekbone or the flush of the cheek.</note> *Feminine, 1st declension.*

<meta>This page continues the anatomical progression from the previous page. 245 **The Stomach** <note>original: *Stomachus* (Lat.), *der Magen* (Ger.), *żołądek* (Pol.)</note> **digests** <note>original: *Concoquo* (Lat.), *verdauen* (Ger.), *trawić* (Pol.). The Latin term *concoquo* literally means "to cook together," reflecting the ancient belief that digestion was a form of internal heat or cooking.</note>.

246 **The Intestines** <note>original: *Intestinum* (Lat.), *das Eingeweide* (Ger.), *kiszka/trzewa* (Pol.)</note> **discharge** <note>original: *Egero* (Lat.), *ausführen* (Ger.), *wyprożniam* (Pol.); refers to the expulsion of waste from the body.</note> waste.

247 **The Liver** <note>original: *Hepar* (Lat.), *die Leber* (Ger.), *wątroba* (Pol.)</note> **produces** <note>original: *Conficio* (Lat.), *zu wege bringen* (Ger.), *sprawię/działam* (Pol.)</note> **the blood** <note>original: *Sangvis* (Lat.), *das Blut* (Ger.), *krew* (Pol.). In Galenic medicine, the liver was thought to be the organ responsible for creating blood from digested food.</note>.

248 It **sends it forth** <note>original: *Dimitto* (Lat.), *schicken* (Ger.), *puszczam* (Pol.)</note> through **the veins** <note>original: *Vena* (Lat.), *die Ader* (Ger.), *żyła* (Pol.)</note>.

249 **The Heart** <note>original: *Cor* (Lat.), *das Herz* (Ger.), *serce* (Pol.)</note> **throbs** <note>original: *Palpito* (Lat.), *klopffen* (Ger.), *drgam* (Pol.)</note>.

250 **The Lungs** <note>original: *Pulmo* (Lat.), *die Lunge* (Ger.), *płuca* (Pol.)</note> **breathe** <note>original: *Respiro* (Lat.), *athemmen* (Ger.), *oddycham* (Pol.)</note>.

251 **The Lip** <note>original: *Labium* (Lat.), *die Lippe* (Ger.), *warga* (Pol.)</note> is **around** <note>original: *Circa* (Lat.), *umb* (Ger.), *około* (Pol.)</note> **the mouth** <note>original: *Os* (Lat.), *der Mund* (Ger.), *gęba/twarz* (Pol.)</note>.

252 **The Beard** <note>original: *Barba* (Lat.), *der Bart* (Ger.), *broda* (Pol.)</note> **adorns** <note>original: *Orno* (Lat.), **zieren** (Ger.), *zdobię* (Pol.)</note> **the chin** <note>original: *Mentum* (Lat.), *das Kinn* (Ger.), *podbrodek* (Pol.)</note>.

253 **The Neck** <note>original: *Collum* (Lat.), *der Hals* (Ger.), *szyja* (Pol.)</note> is **nearest** <note>original: *Proximus* (Lat.), *nächste* (Ger.), *bliski* (Pol.)</note> to the head.

254 **A woman** <note>original: *Foemina* (Lat.), *die Fraw* (Ger.), *niewiasta* (Pol.)</note> is **beardless** <note>original: *Imberbis* (Lat.), *ohne Bart* (Ger.), *bez brody* (Pol.). The Polish gloss *gotowąs* usually refers to a youth whose mustache hasn't grown yet, here used to describe the lack of facial hair.</note>.

255 **The Shoulder** <note>original Latin: *Humerus*; German: *die Schulter*; Polish: *ramię*</note> <term>Masculine noun, 2nd declension</term>.

**The Shoulders** <note>original Latin: *Humeris*</note> <term>The upper joint of the arm</term>.
**The Arms** <note>original Latin: *Brachiis*</note> <term>Specifically the limb from the shoulder to the hand</term>.
**The Hands** <note>original Latin: *Manibus*</note> <term>The primary tools of labor</term>.
**The Left** <note>original Latin: *Siniſtra*</note> <term>Historically associated with holding or stabilizing</term>.
**The Right** <note>original Latin: *Dextra*</note> <term>Historically associated with active work and skill</term>.
**The Palm** <note>original Latin: *Palmâ*</note> <term>The inner surface of the hand</term>.
**The Fist** <note>original Latin: *Pugno*</note> <term>The hand closed tightly for striking</term>.
**The Hollow of the hand** <note>original Latin: *Volâ*</note> <term>The curved interior or "sole" of the hand</term>.
**To carry** <note>original Latin: *bajulámus*</note> <term>To bear a heavy burden</term>.
**To embrace** <note>original Latin: *amplectimur*</note> <term>To encircle with the arms</term>.
**To work** <note>original Latin: *laborámus*</note> <term>To exert physical effort</term>.
**To break** <note>original Latin: *frangimus*</note> <term>To shatter or divide by force</term>.
**To hold** <note>original Latin: *tenet*</note> <term>To keep in one's grasp</term>.

279 **Memory** <note>original: *Memoria, æ.*</note>; the faculty of remembering <note>original German: *das Gedächtniß*; Polish: *pámięć*</note>.
*I remember* <note>original: *Memini*</note>; I am mindful of or I remember <note>original German: *ich bin eingedenck*; Polish: *pámiętam*</note>, a defective verb. <note>A verb that lacks a full set of conjugations; in this case, it only has forms in the perfect tense but with a present meaning.</note>

280 **To forget** <note>original: *Obliviscor, oblitus sum, oblivisci*</note>; to lose from memory <note>original German: *vergessen*; Polish: *zápominam*</note>.
*I recall* <note>original: *Recordor, recordátus sum, recordári*</note>; to remember or mention <note>original German: *sich erinnern*; Polish: *wspominam*</note>.

281 **The Will** <note>original: *Voluntas, átis*</note>; the will <note>original German: *der Wille*; Polish: *wola*</note>.

*To desire* <note>original: *Cupio, cupívi, cupitum, cupere*</note>; to long for <note>original German: *begehren*; Polish: *pożądam*</note>.
**Good** <note>original: *Bonus, a, um*</note>; that which is good <note>original German: *der die das gute*; Polish: *dobry/a/e*</note>.

282 **To turn away** <note>original: *Declino, ávi, átum, áre*</note>; to turn oneself or to withdraw and protect oneself <note>original German: *sich wenden*; Polish: *ustępuie/ chronie sie*</note>.

283 **To deceive** <note>original: *Fallo, fefelli, falsum, fallere*</note>; to cheat or betray <note>original German: *betriegen*; Polish: *zdradzam*</note>.

284 **To rejoice** <note>original: *Gaudeo, gavísus sum, gaudére*</note>; to be glad <note>original German: *sich erfrewen*; Polish: *ráduie sie*</note>.
*To be saddened* <note>original: *Tristor, átus sum, ári*</note>; to be troubled or to grieve <note>original German: *sich betrüben*; Polish: *smece sie*</note>.

285 **Secure** <note>original: *Secúrus, a, um*</note>; he, she, or it that is safe <note>original German: *der die das sichere*; Polish: *bezpieczny/ na/ ne*</note>.
*I care for* <note>original: *Curo, ávi, átum, áre*</note>; to worry about or strive for <note>original German: *sorgen*; Polish: *stáram sie*</note>.

286 **Safe** <note>original: *Tutus, a, um*</note>; he, she, or it that is well-guarded <note>original German: *der die das wolverwahrte*; Polish: *bezpieczny/ na/ ne*</note>.
*I fear* <note>original: *Timeo, ui, ére*</note>; to be afraid or to dread <note>original German: *fürchten*; Polish: *boie sie/ lekam sie*</note>.

287 **Fresh** or **Vigorous** <note>original: *Vegetus, a, um* (Latin); *der die das friſche* (German); *czerſtwy/ wa/ we* (Polish)</note> *adjective of 3 endings.*

288 **To recover** or regain health <note>original: *Convaleſco, convaluí, convaleſcere*; *wieder geſund werden*; *wzmagam/ ozdrawiam*</note> *3rd conjugation, neuter verb.*
